Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, I came because… it’s mostly what’s been happening at church, and I feel embarrassed saying it because I’ve gone there a long time and people know me. I help sometimes with the food after service, you know, the potluck table and napkins and things like that, but lately I keep doing odd things there.

Like I’ll be standing by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up in my hand, just holding it there, and I don’t move for a while. People are waiting, and I know it looks strange. Or I’ll take the same little stack of napkins and fold them over and over, like I’m trying to make them right, but they were already fine. And folks are trying to get past me or waiting on me, and then I notice I’ve been doing it too long.

It’s bothering me because I don’t mean to act that way, and I don’t want people thinking I’m being rude or making trouble. I just sort of get stuck doing these things.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I’m thinking anything clear, doctor. It’s not like I say to myself, “The spoon has to be held just so,” or “These napkins have to be folded this way.” It’s more that I’ll be there by the potluck table, and I’ve got the serving spoon in my hand, and then I just… stay like that. Like I forgot to go on to the next thing.

And with the napkins, I’ll pick up a little stack and fold them, then fold them again, even though they’re already fine. I can see people kind of waiting or needing to get by, and part of me knows I ought to stop and move along, but I keep doing it for a bit. It feels like I get stuck, more than like I have a rule in my head. Then afterward I feel embarrassed because I know it must look odd.

I just sort of get stuck doing these things.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself doing these things, like holding the spoon or folding the napkins, are you thinking something specific or feeling like you have to do it a certain way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’m thinking anything clear, doctor. It’s not like I say to myself, “The spoon has to be held just so,” or “These napkins have to be folded this way.” It’s more that I’ll be there by the potluck table, and I’ve got the serving spoon in my hand, and then I just… stay like that. Like I forgot to go on to the next thing.\n\nAnd with the napkins, I’ll pick up a little stack and fold them, then fold them again, even though they’re already fine. I can see people kind of waiting or needing to get by, and part of me knows I ought to stop and move along, but I keep doing it for a bit. It feels like I get stuck, more than like I have a rule in my head. Then afterward I feel embarrassed because I know it must look odd.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t know about blinking or shoulder shrugging so much, doctor. I can’t say I’ve noticed that kind of thing, or making sounds, not really. What I notice most is what I was saying at church, because that’s where people see it and then I feel ashamed.

I just get stuck there doing something odd, and then afterward I think, “Why did I do that?”

Can you tell me what’s been bothering you or what brings you here today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I came because… it’s mostly what’s been happening at church, and I feel embarrassed saying it because I’ve gone there a long time and people know me. I help sometimes with the food after service, you know, the potluck table and napkins and things like that, but lately I keep doing odd things there.\n\nLike I’ll be standing by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up in my hand, just holding it there, and I don’t move for a while. People are waiting, and I know it looks strange. Or I’ll take the same little stack of napkins and fold them over and over, like I’m trying to make them right, but they were already fine. And folks are trying to get past me or waiting on me, and then I notice I’ve been doing it too long.\n\nIt’s bothering me because I don’t mean to act that way, and I don’t want people thinking I’m being rude or making trouble. I just sort of get stuck doing these things.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself doing these things, like holding the spoon or folding the napkins, are you thinking something specific or feeling like you have to do it a certain way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’m thinking anything clear, doctor. It’s not like I say to myself, “The spoon has to be held just so,” or “These napkins have to be folded this way.” It’s more that I’ll be there by the potluck table, and I’ve got the serving spoon in my hand, and then I just… stay like that. Like I forgot to go on to the next thing.\n\nAnd with the napkins, I’ll pick up a little stack and fold them, then fold them again, even though they’re already fine. I can see people kind of waiting or needing to get by, and part of me knows I ought to stop and move along, but I keep doing it for a bit. It feels like I get stuck, more than like I have a rule in my head. Then afterward I feel embarrassed because I know it must look odd.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you noticed yourself doing other things like blinking your eyes, shrugging your shoulders, or making sounds without meaning to, even when you're not at church or the potluck table?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about blinking or shoulder shrugging so much, doctor. I can’t say I’ve noticed that kind of thing, or making sounds, not really. What I notice most is what I was saying at church, because that’s where people see it and then I feel ashamed.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll be by the potluck table and I’m supposed to be helping, and instead I’m just standing there holding the serving spoon up, not moving on, like my body didn’t go to the next step. Or I’ll keep fussing with the same napkins, folding them over and over while folks are waiting. That’s the sort of thing I mean. It isn’t like I’m trying to do it, and I don’t mean to hold people up. I just get stuck there doing something odd, and then afterward I think, “Why did I do that?”\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore you start doing those things with the spoon or the napkins, do you ever feel a sudden urge or a need to do them, like something inside you is pushing you to act even if you don’t want to?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 71 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior\nmanifestation: \"Doctor, I came because at church I keep doing odd things, like standing by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up and not moving, or folding the same stack of napkins over and over while people are waiting on me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d call it a sudden urge, doctor. It doesn’t feel like something comes over me and says, “Do this now,” exactly. It’s more like I’m already there at the church, by the potluck table, and I’m supposed to be helping, and then I just sort of get caught in it.

Like with the serving spoon, I’ll have it in my hand, maybe I was about to put it down or use it, and then I’m just standing there holding it up. People might be waiting for the food, and I can see them, but I don’t move right away. Or with the napkins, I’ll take the same little stack and keep folding them over and over, even though there’s no real reason for it. I don’t feel like I want to do it, but I don’t stop when I should either.

So, no, not like a strong need beforehand that I can name. It’s more that I get stuck doing these odd little things, and then I realize people are watching or waiting on me, and I feel embarrassed.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t usually know before, no. I don’t get much warning like, “Oh, I’m going to do that odd thing again.” It’s more after I’m already there, you know, standing by the church potluck table, and then I notice I’ve got the serving spoon held up and I’m not moving along like I ought to.

Or I’ll be folding the napkins, and then somehow it’s the same little stack, over and over, and people are waiting on me or trying to get around me. That’s when it kind of hits me that I’m stuck doing something strange. I came because of that, really, because it keeps happening at church and it embarrasses me. Before it happens, I don’t feel much of anything special that tells me it’s coming.

It’s not like I wake up and think, “Today I’m going to get stuck at the potluck table.” It’s more that when I’m there at church, especially if I’m helping with the food and people are moving around me, I can feel kind of... off, I guess. Like I’m not keeping up right.

Then I’ll catch myself just standing there with the serving spoon held up, not putting it down, not serving, not doing the next thing. Or I’ll be folding that same stack of napkins again and again while folks are waiting, and I know it must look strange. That’s really why I came, because I keep doing these odd things there and it embarrasses me. I don’t always sense it before it happens, but once I’m in it, I can tell something isn’t right.

I don’t really notice it so much away from church, doctor. Not like tapping my fingers or rocking, no, not that I can think of. The thing that made me come in is mostly what happens there, when I’m helping at the potluck table.

I’ll be standing there with the serving spoon, and instead of just serving the food or setting it down like a normal person would, I’m just holding it up and not moving on. And people are there waiting, you know, with their plates, and I can feel that I’m holding everything up but I still don’t seem to get myself going right away.

Or with the napkins, I’ll have that same little stack and I keep folding them over and over, like I’m doing a job, but really I’m not helping anymore. I’m just repeating it while folks are trying to get around me. That’s the part that embarrasses me so much, because it’s right there in front of people I know from church. Away from there, I can’t say I’ve noticed the same kind of thing the way I have with the spoon and the napkins.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not so much at home or other places that I can point to, doctor. I mean, I get slow with things sometimes, but the thing that really made me come in is what happens at church, right there where everybody can see me.

It’s mostly at the potluck table. I’ll be helping, or trying to help, and then I’m just standing there with the serving spoon held up, like I’m about to serve something, but I don’t move on. People are waiting with their plates, and I know I’m in the way, but I sort of stay there too long.

And the napkins too — I’ll take the same little stack and fold them again and again, like I’m doing something useful, but really I’m just stuck on it while folks are trying to get around me. That’s the embarrassing part. It’s not that I’m trying to act odd. I just find myself doing it, and then I realize people are noticing. That’s mostly why I came.

I help sometimes with the food after service, you know, the potluck table and napkins and things like that, but lately I keep doing odd things there.\n\nLike I’ll be standing by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up in my hand, just holding it there, and I don’t move for a while. I just sort of get stuck doing these things.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself doing these things, like holding the spoon or folding the napkins, are you thinking something specific or feeling like you have to do it a certain way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’m thinking anything clear, doctor. It’s not like I say to myself, “The spoon has to be held just so,” or “These napkins have to be folded this way.” It’s more that I’ll be there by the potluck table, and I’ve got the serving spoon in my hand, and then I just… stay like that. Like I forgot to go on to the next thing.\n\nAnd with the napkins, I’ll pick up a little stack and fold them, then fold them again, even though they’re already fine. I can see people kind of waiting or needing to get by, and part of me knows I ought to stop and move along, but I keep doing it for a bit. It feels like I get stuck, more than like I have a rule in my head. Then afterward I feel embarrassed because I know it must look odd.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you noticed yourself doing other things like blinking your eyes, shrugging your shoulders, or making sounds without meaning to, even when you're not at church or the potluck table?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about blinking or shoulder shrugging so much, doctor. I can’t say I’ve noticed that kind of thing, or making sounds, not really. What I notice most is what I was saying at church, because that’s where people see it and then I feel ashamed.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll be by the potluck table and I’m supposed to be helping, and instead I’m just standing there holding the serving spoon up, not moving on, like my body didn’t go to the next step. Or I’ll keep fussing with the same napkins, folding them over and over while folks are waiting. That’s the sort of thing I mean. It isn’t like I’m trying to do it, and I don’t mean to hold people up. I just get stuck there doing something odd, and then afterward I think, “Why did I do that?”\n\nDoctor: Before you start doing those things with the spoon or the napkins, do you ever feel a sudden urge or a need to do them, like something inside you is pushing you to act even if you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a sudden urge, doctor. It doesn’t feel like something comes over me and says, “Do this now,” exactly. It’s more like I’m already there at the church, by the potluck table, and I’m supposed to be helping, and then I just sort of get caught in it.\n\nLike with the serving spoon, I’ll have it in my hand, maybe I was about to put it down or use it, and then I’m just standing there holding it up. People might be waiting for the food, and I can see them, but I don’t move right away. Or with the napkins, I’ll take the same little stack and keep folding them over and over, even though there’s no real reason for it. I don’t feel like I want to do it, but I don’t stop when I should either.\n\nSo, no, not like a strong need beforehand that I can name. It’s more that I get stuck doing these odd little things, and then I realize people are watching or waiting on me, and I feel embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Before you start doing those things at the potluck table, like holding the spoon or folding the napkins, do you ever have a feeling that you might do something unusual, or do you ever sense that you might get stuck before it actually happens?\n\nPatient: I don’t usually know before, no. I don’t get much warning like, “Oh, I’m going to do that odd thing again.” It’s more after I’m already there, you know, standing by the church potluck table, and then I notice I’ve got the serving spoon held up and I’m not moving along like I ought to.\n\nOr I’ll be folding the napkins, and then somehow it’s the same little stack, over and over, and people are waiting on me or trying to get around me. That’s when it kind of hits me that I’m stuck doing something strange. I came because of that, really, because it keeps happening at church and it embarrasses me. Before it happens, I don’t feel much of anything special that tells me it’s coming.\n\nDoctor: Before you start doing those things at the potluck table, like holding the spoon or folding the napkins, do you ever feel like something isn’t quite right or have a sense that you might get stuck, even just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but not in a clear way, doctor. It’s not like I wake up and think, “Today I’m going to get stuck at the potluck table.” It’s more that when I’m there at church, especially if I’m helping with the food and people are moving around me, I can feel kind of... off, I guess. Like I’m not keeping up right.\n\nThen I’ll catch myself just standing there with the serving spoon held up, not putting it down, not serving, not doing the next thing.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think I’m aware, doctor… at least some of the time. It’s not like I’m completely gone or asleep or anything like that. I can see the people there with their plates, and I know I’m at the church potluck table, and I know I’m supposed to be helping. That’s what makes it so embarrassing, because I’m not unaware that folks are waiting on me.

It’s just that I get stuck in the action. Like I’ll be holding the serving spoon up, just sort of paused there, and I know I should either serve the food or put the spoon down, but I don’t move right away. Or with the napkins, I’ll be folding the same little stack over and over, and part of me knows, “You already did that,” but I keep doing it anyway. I can tell people are trying to get around me, and then I feel ashamed because I know it must look odd.

So I wouldn’t say I’m fully spaced out. It’s more like I’m there, but I’m not moving along the way I’m supposed to.

No, I don’t think so, doctor. Not outside of church, not in that way you’re saying. I don’t have a thought like, “If I don’t do this three times something bad will happen,” or anything like that. At least I haven’t noticed that.

It’s more like, when it happens at the church table, I just get stuck doing it or holding still too long. But at home, I can’t say I’m driven to repeat things because I’m scared something will happen. I mean, I may check myself with ordinary things like anyone does, but not over and over because I feel I have to do it just right. I don’t really have an example of that.

So I guess the answer is no, not that I know of.

It doesn’t feel like something comes over me and says, “Do this now,” exactly. It’s more like I’m already there at the church, by the potluck table, and I’m supposed to be helping, and then I just sort of get caught in it.\n\nLike with the serving spoon, I’ll have it in my hand, maybe I was about to put it down or use it, and then I’m just standing there holding it up. People might be waiting for the food, and I can see them, but I don’t move right away. Or with the napkins, I’ll take the same little stack and keep folding them over and over, even though there’s no real reason for it. I don’t feel like I want to do it, but I don’t stop when I should either.\n\nSo, no, not like a strong need beforehand that I can name. It’s more that I get stuck doing these odd little things, and then I realize people are watching or waiting on me, and I feel embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Before you start doing those things at the potluck table, like holding the spoon or folding the napkins, do you ever have a feeling that you might do something unusual, or do you ever sense that you might get stuck before it actually happens?\n\nPatient: I don’t usually know before, no. I don’t get much warning like, “Oh, I’m going to do that odd thing again.” It’s more after I’m already there, you know, standing by the church potluck table, and then I notice I’ve got the serving spoon held up and I’m not moving along like I ought to.\n\nOr I’ll be folding the napkins, and then somehow it’s the same little stack, over and over, and people are waiting on me or trying to get around me. That’s when it kind of hits me that I’m stuck doing something strange. I came because of that, really, because it keeps happening at church and it embarrasses me. Before it happens, I don’t feel much of anything special that tells me it’s coming.\n\nDoctor: Before you start doing those things at the potluck table, like holding the spoon or folding the napkins, do you ever feel like something isn’t quite right or have a sense that you might get stuck, even just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but not in a clear way, doctor. It’s not like I wake up and think, “Today I’m going to get stuck at the potluck table.” It’s more that when I’m there at church, especially if I’m helping with the food and people are moving around me, I can feel kind of... off, I guess. Like I’m not keeping up right.\n\nThen I’ll catch myself just standing there with the serving spoon held up, not putting it down, not serving, not doing the next thing. Or I’ll be folding that same stack of napkins again and again while folks are waiting, and I know it must look strange. That’s really why I came, because I keep doing these odd things there and it embarrasses me. I don’t always sense it before it happens, but once I’m in it, I can tell something isn’t right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself making repeated movements or doing the same thing over and over again when you're not at church, like tapping your fingers, rocking back and forth, or anything else that you can't seem to stop once it starts?\n\nPatient: I don’t really notice it so much away from church, doctor. Not like tapping my fingers or rocking, no, not that I can think of. The thing that made me come in is mostly what happens there, when I’m helping at the potluck table.\n\nI’ll be standing there with the serving spoon, and instead of just serving the food or setting it down like a normal person would, I’m just holding it up and not moving on. And people are there waiting, you know, with their plates, and I can feel that I’m holding everything up but I still don’t seem to get myself going right away.\n\nOr with the napkins, I’ll have that same little stack and I keep folding them over and over, like I’m doing a job, but really I’m not helping anymore. I’m just repeating it while folks are trying to get around me. That’s the part that embarrasses me so much, because it’s right there in front of people I know from church. Away from there, I can’t say I’ve noticed the same kind of thing the way I have with the spoon and the napkins.\n\nDoctor: Outside of what happens at church, have you noticed any problems at home, work, or in other situations where you feel stuck doing things over and over, or where your behavior has caused you embarrassment or trouble?\n\nPatient: Not so much at home or other places that I can point to, doctor. I mean, I get slow with things sometimes, but the thing that really made me come in is what happens at church, right there where everybody can see me.\n\nIt’s mostly at the potluck table. I’ll be helping, or trying to help, and then I’m just standing there with the serving spoon held up, like I’m about to serve something, but I don’t move on. People are waiting with their plates, and I know I’m in the way, but I sort of stay there too long.\n\nAnd the napkins too — I’ll take the same little stack and fold them again and again, like I’m doing something useful, but really I’m just stuck on it while folks are trying to get around me. That’s the embarrassing part. It’s not that I’m trying to act odd. I just find myself doing it, and then I realize people are noticing. That’s mostly why I came.\n\nDoctor: When you're holding the serving spoon or folding the napkins and you feel stuck, are you aware of what's going on around you, or does it feel like you're spaced out or not fully present?\n\nPatient: I think I’m aware, doctor… at least some of the time. It’s not like I’m completely gone or asleep or anything like that. I can see the people there with their plates, and I know I’m at the church potluck table, and I know I’m supposed to be helping. That’s what makes it so embarrassing, because I’m not unaware that folks are waiting on me.\n\nIt’s just that I get stuck in the action. Like I’ll be holding the serving spoon up, just sort of paused there, and I know I should either serve the food or put the spoon down, but I don’t move right away. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… not really like my face twitching or my hands doing little movements when I’m just sitting at home, if that’s what you mean. I can’t say I’ve noticed that happening away from church.

The thing I keep trying to explain is more what happens there, at the church potluck table. I’ll be standing there with the serving spoon up in my hand, like I’m fixing to serve somebody, and then I just don’t go on with it. I’m just stuck there, and people are waiting. Or I’ll take that same little stack of napkins and fold them over and over, even though they’re already folded, and I know folks are trying to get by me.

So it’s not that I’m always making little movements all over the place, doctor. It’s more those odd things at church, where I get caught doing them and it embarrasses me.

And people are there waiting, you know, with their plates, and I can feel that I’m holding everything up but I still don’t seem to get myself going right away.\n\nOr with the napkins, I’ll have that same little stack and I keep folding them over and over, like I’m doing a job, but really I’m not helping anymore. I’m just repeating it while folks are trying to get around me. That’s the part that embarrasses me so much, because it’s right there in front of people I know from church. Away from there, I can’t say I’ve noticed the same kind of thing the way I have with the spoon and the napkins.\n\nDoctor: Outside of what happens at church, have you noticed any problems at home, work, or in other situations where you feel stuck doing things over and over, or where your behavior has caused you embarrassment or trouble?\n\nPatient: Not so much at home or other places that I can point to, doctor. I mean, I get slow with things sometimes, but the thing that really made me come in is what happens at church, right there where everybody can see me.\n\nIt’s mostly at the potluck table. I’ll be helping, or trying to help, and then I’m just standing there with the serving spoon held up, like I’m about to serve something, but I don’t move on. People are waiting with their plates, and I know I’m in the way, but I sort of stay there too long.\n\nAnd the napkins too — I’ll take the same little stack and fold them again and again, like I’m doing something useful, but really I’m just stuck on it while folks are trying to get around me. That’s the embarrassing part. It’s not that I’m trying to act odd. I just find myself doing it, and then I realize people are noticing. That’s mostly why I came.\n\nDoctor: When you're holding the serving spoon or folding the napkins and you feel stuck, are you aware of what's going on around you, or does it feel like you're spaced out or not fully present?\n\nPatient: I think I’m aware, doctor… at least some of the time. It’s not like I’m completely gone or asleep or anything like that. I can see the people there with their plates, and I know I’m at the church potluck table, and I know I’m supposed to be helping. That’s what makes it so embarrassing, because I’m not unaware that folks are waiting on me.\n\nIt’s just that I get stuck in the action. I mean, I may check myself with ordinary things like anyone does, but not over and over because I feel I have to do it just right. I don’t really have an example of that.\n\nSo I guess the answer is no, not that I know of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself making small, repeated movements with your hands, face, or other parts of your body when you're not at church, things you can't quite control even if you try?\n\nPatient: Well… not really like my face twitching or my hands doing little movements when I’m just sitting at home, if that’s what you mean. I can’t say I’ve noticed that happening away from church.\n\nThe thing I keep trying to explain is more what happens there, at the church potluck table. It’s more those odd things at church, where I get caught doing them and it embarrasses me.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you start folding the napkins or holding the serving spoon, do you feel like you can't stop even if you want to, or does it feel more like you just happen to keep doing it without really trying to?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 71 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior\nmanifestation: \"Doctor, I came because at church I keep doing odd things, like standing by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up and not moving, or folding the same stack of napkins over and over while people are waiting on me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It feels more like I just get caught in it, doctor. I don’t start out thinking, “I have to fold these napkins and I can’t stop,” exactly. It’s not like I’m choosing to do it over and over, but it’s not like I’m fully deciding either.

Like at the potluck table, I’ll pick up the napkins because I’m supposed to be helping, and then I’ll fold that same little stack again, and then maybe again, and I know people are there waiting or trying to reach around me. Or with the serving spoon, I’ll have it up in my hand, ready to put food on somebody’s plate, and then I just sort of stand there not moving on. I can feel that I ought to stop or go to the next thing, but I don’t right away.

So… I wouldn’t say it feels like something bad will happen if I stop. It’s more that I happen to keep doing it, or I freeze there, and then I notice everybody waiting and I feel foolish.

I help sometimes with the food after service, you know, the potluck table and napkins and things like that, but lately I keep doing odd things there.\n\nLike I’ll be standing by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up in my hand, just holding it there, and I don’t move for a while. People are waiting, and I know it looks strange. Or I’ll take the same little stack of napkins and fold them over and over, like I’m trying to make them right, but they were already fine. And folks are trying to get past me or waiting on me, and then I notice I’ve been doing it too long.\n\nIt’s bothering me because I don’t mean to act that way, and I don’t want people thinking I’m being rude or making trouble. I just sort of get stuck doing these things.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself doing these things, like holding the spoon or folding the napkins, are you thinking something specific or feeling like you have to do it a certain way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’m thinking anything clear, doctor. It’s not like I say to myself, “The spoon has to be held just so,” or “These napkins have to be folded this way.” It’s more that I’ll be there by the potluck table, and I’ve got the serving spoon in my hand, and then I just… stay like that. Like I forgot to go on to the next thing.\n\nAnd with the napkins, I’ll pick up a little stack and fold them, then fold them again, even though they’re already fine. I can’t say I’ve noticed that kind of thing, or making sounds, not really. What I notice most is what I was saying at church, because that’s where people see it and then I feel ashamed.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll be by the potluck table and I’m supposed to be helping, and instead I’m just standing there holding the serving spoon up, not moving on, like my body didn’t go to the next step. Or I’ll keep fussing with the same napkins, folding them over and over while folks are waiting. That’s the sort of thing I mean. It isn’t like I’m trying to do it, and I don’t mean to hold people up. I just get stuck there doing something odd, and then afterward I think, “Why did I do that?”\n\nDoctor: Before you start doing those things with the spoon or the napkins, do you ever feel a sudden urge or a need to do them, like something inside you is pushing you to act even if you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a sudden urge, doctor. It doesn’t feel like something comes over me and says, “Do this now,” exactly. It’s more like I’m already there at the church, by the potluck table, and I’m supposed to be helping, and then I just sort of get caught in it.\n\nLike with the serving spoon, I’ll have it in my hand, maybe I was about to put it down or use it, and then I’m just standing there holding it up. People might be waiting for the food, and I can see them, but I don’t move right away. Or with the napkins, I’ll take the same little stack and keep folding them over and over, even though there’s no real reason for it. I don’t feel like I want to do it, but I don’t stop when I should either.\n\nSo, no, not like a strong need beforehand that I can name. It’s more that I get stuck doing these odd little things, and then I realize people are watching or waiting on me, and I feel embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Before you start doing those things at the potluck table, like holding the spoon or folding the napkins, do you ever have a feeling that you might do something unusual, or do you ever sense that you might get stuck before it actually happens?\n\nPatient: I don’t usually know before, no. I don’t get much warning like, “Oh, I’m going to do that odd thing again.” It’s more after I’m already there, you know, standing by the church potluck table, and then I notice I’ve got the serving spoon held up and I’m not moving along like I ought to.\n\nOr I’ll be folding the napkins, and then somehow it’s the same little stack, over and over, and people are waiting on me or trying to get around me. That’s when it kind of hits me that I’m stuck doing something strange. I came because of that, really, because it keeps happening at church and it embarrasses me. Before it happens, I don’t feel much of anything special that tells me it’s coming.\n\nDoctor: Before you start doing those things at the potluck table, like holding the spoon or folding the napkins, do you ever feel like something isn’t quite right or have a sense that you might get stuck, even just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but not in a clear way, doctor. It’s not like I wake up and think, “Today I’m going to get stuck at the potluck table.” It’s more that when I’m there at church, especially if I’m helping with the food and people are moving around me, I can feel kind of... off, I guess. Like I’m not keeping up right.\n\nThen I’ll catch myself just standing there with the serving spoon held up, not putting it down, not serving, not doing the next thing. Or I’ll be folding that same stack of napkins again and again while folks are waiting, and I know it must look strange. That’s really why I came, because I keep doing these odd things there and it embarrasses me. I don’t always sense it before it happens, but once I’m in it, I can tell something isn’t right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself making repeated movements or doing the same thing over and over again when you're not at church, like tapping your fingers, rocking back and forth, or anything else that you can't seem to stop once it starts?\n\nPatient: I don’t really notice it so much away from church, doctor. Not like tapping my fingers or rocking, no, not that I can think of. The thing that made me come in is mostly what happens there, when I’m helping at the potluck table.\n\nI’ll be standing there with the serving spoon, and instead of just serving the food or setting it down like a normal person would, I’m just holding it up and not moving on. And people are there waiting, you know, with their plates, and I can feel that I’m holding everything up but I still don’t seem to get myself going right away.\n\nOr with the napkins, I’ll have that same little stack and I keep folding them over and over, like I’m doing a job, but really I’m not helping anymore. I’m just repeating it while folks are trying to get around me. That’s the part that embarrasses me so much, because it’s right there in front of people I know from church. Away from there, I can’t say I’ve noticed the same kind of thing the way I have with the spoon and the napkins.\n\nDoctor: Outside of what happens at church, have you noticed any problems at home, work, or in other situations where you feel stuck doing things over and over, or where your behavior has caused you embarrassment or trouble?\n\nPatient: Not so much at home or other places that I can point to, doctor. I mean, I get slow with things sometimes, but the thing that really made me come in is what happens at church, right there where everybody can see me.\n\nIt’s mostly at the potluck table. I’ll be helping, or trying to help, and then I’m just standing there with the serving spoon held up, like I’m about to serve something, but I don’t move on. People are waiting with their plates, and I know I’m in the way, but I sort of stay there too long.\n\nAnd the napkins too — I’ll take the same little stack and fold them again and again, like I’m doing something useful, but really I’m just stuck on it while folks are trying to get around me. That’s the embarrassing part. It’s not that I’m trying to act odd. I just find myself doing it, and then I realize people are noticing. That’s mostly why I came.\n\nDoctor: When you're holding the serving spoon or folding the napkins and you feel stuck, are you aware of what's going on around you, or does it feel like you're spaced out or not fully present?\n\nPatient: I think I’m aware, doctor… at least some of the time. It’s not like I’m completely gone or asleep or anything like that. I can see the people there with their plates, and I know I’m at the church potluck table, and I know I’m supposed to be helping. That’s what makes it so embarrassing, because I’m not unaware that folks are waiting on me.\n\nIt’s just that I get stuck in the action. Like I’ll be holding the serving spoon up, just sort of paused there, and I know I should either serve the food or put the spoon down, but I don’t move right away. Or with the napkins, I’ll be folding the same little stack over and over, and part of me knows, “You already did that,” but I keep doing it anyway. I can tell people are trying to get around me, and then I feel ashamed because I know it must look odd.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m fully spaced out. It’s more like I’m there, but I’m not moving along the way I’m supposed to.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Outside of church, have you ever felt driven to repeat certain actions over and over because you felt like something bad would happen if you didn’t, or because it just felt like you had to do it a certain way?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so, doctor. I don’t really have an example of that.\n\nSo I guess the answer is no, not that I know of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself making small, repeated movements with your hands, face, or other parts of your body when you're not at church, things you can't quite control even if you try?\n\nPatient: Well… not really like my face twitching or my hands doing little movements when I’m just sitting at home, if that’s what you mean. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I’d call it a strong need, doctor. It’s not like there’s a voice in me saying, “Keep folding those napkins,” or “Don’t put that spoon down.” It’s more… I get stuck in the middle of what I’m doing.

Like at church, when I’m by the potluck table, I’ll be holding the serving spoon up, ready to put something on somebody’s plate, and then I just stand there. I can see the person waiting, and I know I’m supposed to move, but I don’t move right away. Or with the napkins, I’ll have the same little stack in my hands and I’ll fold them over and over, even though there’s no reason to keep doing it. People are trying to get their food or get around me, and I know I’m acting odd, but I sort of keep on or freeze there.

So it isn’t exactly pressure like I have to do it to feel better. It’s more like my body or my mind doesn’t go on to the next step the way it should, and then I feel embarrassed because everybody is waiting on me.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think it starts like a sudden urge, no. Not like I’m sitting there and then all at once something inside shoves me to pick up the spoon or grab the napkins. It’s more that I’m already there at church, trying to help at the potluck table, and then I end up doing something odd.

Like I’ll have the serving spoon in my hand, and I’m supposed to be putting food on somebody’s plate, and instead I just hold it up there and don’t move on. Or I’ll get hold of the same little stack of napkins and fold them again and again, even though they’re already folded, and people are waiting on me or trying to get by.

So before it starts, I can’t really say I feel some big impulse I can’t control. It’s more like I slip into it, and then I notice I’m standing there or repeating it, and by then I feel embarrassed because folks are looking and waiting.

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F06.8, F32.9, F43.21\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, thank you for coming in. Can you tell me what’s been bothering you or what brings you here today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I came because… it’s mostly what’s been happening at church, and I feel embarrassed saying it because I’ve gone there a long time and people know me. I help sometimes with the food after service, you know, the potluck table and napkins and things like that, but lately I keep doing odd things there.\n\nLike I’ll be standing by the potluck table holding the serving spoon up in my hand, just holding it there, and I don’t move for a while. People are waiting, and I know it looks strange. Or I’ll take the same little stack of napkins and fold them over and over, like I’m trying to make them right, but they were already fine. And folks are trying to get past me or waiting on me, and then I notice I’ve been doing it too long.\n\nIt’s bothering me because I don’t mean to act that way, and I don’t want people thinking I’m being rude or making trouble. I just sort of get stuck doing these things.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself doing these things, like holding the spoon or folding the napkins, are you thinking something specific or feeling like you have to do it a certain way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’m thinking anything clear, doctor. It’s not like I say to myself, “The spoon has to be held just so,” or “These napkins have to be folded this way.” It’s more that I’ll be there by the potluck table, and I’ve got the serving spoon in my hand, and then I just… stay like that. Like I forgot to go on to the next thing.\n\nAnd with the napkins, I’ll pick up a little stack and fold them, then fold them again, even though they’re already fine. I can see people kind of waiting or needing to get by, and part of me knows I ought to stop and move along, but I keep doing it for a bit. It feels like I get stuck, more than like I have a rule in my head. Then afterward I feel embarrassed because I know it must look odd.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Have you noticed yourself doing other things like blinking your eyes, shrugging your shoulders, or making sounds without meaning to, even when you're not at church or the potluck table?\n\nPatient: I don’t know about blinking or shoulder shrugging so much, doctor. I can’t say I’ve noticed that kind of thing, or making sounds, not really. What I notice most is what I was saying at church, because that’s where people see it and then I feel ashamed.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll be by the potluck table and I’m supposed to be helping, and instead I’m just standing there holding the serving spoon up, not moving on, like my body didn’t go to the next step. Or I’ll keep fussing with the same napkins, folding them over and over while folks are waiting. That’s the sort of thing I mean. It isn’t like I’m trying to do it, and I don’t mean to hold people up. I just get stuck there doing something odd, and then afterward I think, “Why did I do that?”\n\nDoctor: Before you start doing those things with the spoon or the napkins, do you ever feel a sudden urge or a need to do them, like something inside you is pushing you to act even if you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it a sudden urge, doctor. It doesn’t feel like something comes over me and says, “Do this now,” exactly. It’s more like I’m already there at the church, by the potluck table, and I’m supposed to be helping, and then I just sort of get caught in it.\n\nLike with the serving spoon, I’ll have it in my hand, maybe I was about to put it down or use it, and then I’m just standing there holding it up. People might be waiting for the food, and I can see them, but I don’t move right away. Or with the napkins, I’ll take the same little stack and keep folding them over and over, even though there’s no real reason for it. I don’t feel like I want to do it, but I don’t stop when I should either.\n\nSo, no, not like a strong need beforehand that I can name. It’s more that I get stuck doing these odd little things, and then I realize people are watching or waiting on me, and I feel embarrassed.\n\nDoctor: Before you start doing those things at the potluck table, like holding the spoon or folding the napkins, do you ever have a feeling that you might do something unusual, or do you ever sense that you might get stuck before it actually happens?\n\nPatient: I don’t usually know before, no. I don’t get much warning like, “Oh, I’m going to do that odd thing again.” It’s more after I’m already there, you know, standing by the church potluck table, and then I notice I’ve got the serving spoon held up and I’m not moving along like I ought to.\n\nOr I’ll be folding the napkins, and then somehow it’s the same little stack, over and over, and people are waiting on me or trying to get around me. That’s when it kind of hits me that I’m stuck doing something strange. I came because of that, really, because it keeps happening at church and it embarrasses me. Before it happens, I don’t feel much of anything special that tells me it’s coming.\n\nDoctor: Before you start doing those things at the potluck table, like holding the spoon or folding the napkins, do you ever feel like something isn’t quite right or have a sense that you might get stuck, even just a little bit?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but not in a clear way, doctor. It’s not like I wake up and think, “Today I’m going to get stuck at the potluck table.” It’s more that when I’m there at church, especially if I’m helping with the food and people are moving around me, I can feel kind of... off, I guess. Like I’m not keeping up right.\n\nThen I’ll catch myself just standing there with the serving spoon held up, not putting it down, not serving, not doing the next thing. Or I’ll be folding that same stack of napkins again and again while folks are waiting, and I know it must look strange. That’s really why I came, because I keep doing these odd things there and it embarrasses me. I don’t always sense it before it happens, but once I’m in it, I can tell something isn’t right.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself making repeated movements or doing the same thing over and over again when you're not at church, like tapping your fingers, rocking back and forth, or anything else that you can't seem to stop once it starts?\n\nPatient: I don’t really notice it so much away from church, doctor. Not like tapping my fingers or rocking, no, not that I can think of. The thing that made me come in is mostly what happens there, when I’m helping at the potluck table.\n\nI’ll be standing there with the serving spoon, and instead of just serving the food or setting it down like a normal person would, I’m just holding it up and not moving on. And people are there waiting, you know, with their plates, and I can feel that I’m holding everything up but I still don’t seem to get myself going right away.\n\nOr with the napkins, I’ll have that same little stack and I keep folding them over and over, like I’m doing a job, but really I’m not helping anymore. I’m just repeating it while folks are trying to get around me. That’s the part that embarrasses me so much, because it’s right there in front of people I know from church. Away from there, I can’t say I’ve noticed the same kind of thing the way I have with the spoon and the napkins.\n\nDoctor: Outside of what happens at church, have you noticed any problems at home, work, or in other situations where you feel stuck doing things over and over, or where your behavior has caused you embarrassment or trouble?\n\nPatient: Not so much at home or other places that I can point to, doctor. I mean, I get slow with things sometimes, but the thing that really made me come in is what happens at church, right there where everybody can see me.\n\nIt’s mostly at the potluck table. I’ll be helping, or trying to help, and then I’m just standing there with the serving spoon held up, like I’m about to serve something, but I don’t move on. People are waiting with their plates, and I know I’m in the way, but I sort of stay there too long.\n\nAnd the napkins too — I’ll take the same little stack and fold them again and again, like I’m doing something useful, but really I’m just stuck on it while folks are trying to get around me. That’s the embarrassing part. It’s not that I’m trying to act odd. I just find myself doing it, and then I realize people are noticing. That’s mostly why I came.\n\nDoctor: When you're holding the serving spoon or folding the napkins and you feel stuck, are you aware of what's going on around you, or does it feel like you're spaced out or not fully present?\n\nPatient: I think I’m aware, doctor… at least some of the time. It’s not like I’m completely gone or asleep or anything like that. I can see the people there with their plates, and I know I’m at the church potluck table, and I know I’m supposed to be helping. That’s what makes it so embarrassing, because I’m not unaware that folks are waiting on me.\n\nIt’s just that I get stuck in the action. Like I’ll be holding the serving spoon up, just sort of paused there, and I know I should either serve the food or put the spoon down, but I don’t move right away. Or with the napkins, I’ll be folding the same little stack over and over, and part of me knows, “You already did that,” but I keep doing it anyway. I can tell people are trying to get around me, and then I feel ashamed because I know it must look odd.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m fully spaced out. It’s more like I’m there, but I’m not moving along the way I’m supposed to.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Outside of church, have you ever felt driven to repeat certain actions over and over because you felt like something bad would happen if you didn’t, or because it just felt like you had to do it a certain way?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so, doctor. Not outside of church, not in that way you’re saying. I don’t have a thought like, “If I don’t do this three times something bad will happen,” or anything like that. At least I haven’t noticed that.\n\nIt’s more like, when it happens at the church table, I just get stuck doing it or holding still too long. But at home, I can’t say I’m driven to repeat things because I’m scared something will happen. I mean, I may check myself with ordinary things like anyone does, but not over and over because I feel I have to do it just right. I don’t really have an example of that.\n\nSo I guess the answer is no, not that I know of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself making small, repeated movements with your hands, face, or other parts of your body when you're not at church, things you can't quite control even if you try?\n\nPatient: Well… not really like my face twitching or my hands doing little movements when I’m just sitting at home, if that’s what you mean. I can’t say I’ve noticed that happening away from church.\n\nThe thing I keep trying to explain is more what happens there, at the church potluck table. I’ll be standing there with the serving spoon up in my hand, like I’m fixing to serve somebody, and then I just don’t go on with it. I’m just stuck there, and people are waiting. Or I’ll take that same little stack of napkins and fold them over and over, even though they’re already folded, and I know folks are trying to get by me.\n\nSo it’s not that I’m always making little movements all over the place, doctor. It’s more those odd things at church, where I get caught doing them and it embarrasses me.\n\nDoctor: When you start folding the napkins or holding the serving spoon, do you feel like you can't stop even if you want to, or does it feel more like you just happen to keep doing it without really trying to?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I just get caught in it, doctor. I don’t start out thinking, “I have to fold these napkins and I can’t stop,” exactly. It’s not like I’m choosing to do it over and over, but it’s not like I’m fully deciding either.\n\nLike at the potluck table, I’ll pick up the napkins because I’m supposed to be helping, and then I’ll fold that same little stack again, and then maybe again, and I know people are there waiting or trying to reach around me. Or with the serving spoon, I’ll have it up in my hand, ready to put food on somebody’s plate, and then I just sort of stand there not moving on. I can feel that I ought to stop or go to the next thing, but I don’t right away.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say it feels like something bad will happen if I stop. It’s more that I happen to keep doing it, or I freeze there, and then I notice everybody waiting and I feel foolish.\n\nDoctor: When you start folding the napkins or holding the serving spoon, do you feel an internal pressure or a strong need to keep doing it, even if you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a strong need, doctor. It’s not like there’s a voice in me saying, “Keep folding those napkins,” or “Don’t put that spoon down.” It’s more… I get stuck in the middle of what I’m doing.\n\nLike at church, when I’m by the potluck table, I’ll be holding the serving spoon up, ready to put something on somebody’s plate, and then I just stand there. I can see the person waiting, and I know I’m supposed to move, but I don’t move right away. Or with the napkins, I’ll have the same little stack in my hands and I’ll fold them over and over, even though there’s no reason to keep doing it. People are trying to get their food or get around me, and I know I’m acting odd, but I sort of keep on or freeze there.\n\nSo it isn’t exactly pressure like I have to do it to feel better. It’s more like my body or my mind doesn’t go on to the next step the way it should, and then I feel embarrassed because everybody is waiting on me.\n\nDoctor: Before you start holding the spoon up or folding the napkins over and over, do you ever feel a sudden, strong urge or impulse that you can't control, like something inside is pushing you to start the action even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it starts like a sudden urge, no. Not like I’m sitting there and then all at once something inside shoves me to pick up the spoon or grab the napkins. It’s more that I’m already there at church, trying to help at the potluck table, and then I end up doing something odd.\n\nLike I’ll have the serving spoon in my hand, and I’m supposed to be putting food on somebody’s plate, and instead I just hold it up there and don’t move on. Or I’ll get hold of the same little stack of napkins and fold them again and again, even though they’re already folded, and people are waiting on me or trying to get by.\n\nSo before it starts, I can’t really say I feel some big impulse I can’t control. It’s more like I’m there, but I’m not moving along the way I’m supposed to.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Outside of church, have you ever felt driven to repeat certain actions over and over because you felt like something bad would happen if you didn’t, or because it just felt like you had to do it a certain way?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so, doctor. Not outside of church, not in that way you’re saying. I don’t have a thought like, “If I don’t do this three times something bad will happen,” or anything like that. At least I haven’t noticed that.\n\nIt’s more like, when it happens at the church table, I just get stuck doing it or holding still too long. But at home, I can’t say I’m driven to repeat things because I’m scared something will happen. I mean, I may check myself with ordinary things like anyone does, but not over and over because I feel I have to do it just right. I don’t really have an example of that.\n\nSo I guess the answer is no, not that I know of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself making small, repeated movements with your hands, face, or other parts of your body when you're not at church, things you can't quite control even if you try?\n\nPatient: Well… not really like my face twitching or my hands doing little movements when I’m just sitting at home, if that’s what you mean. I can’t say I’ve noticed that happening away from church.\n\nThe thing I keep trying to explain is more what happens there, at the church potluck table. I’ll be standing there with the serving spoon up in my hand, like I’m fixing to serve somebody, and then I just don’t go on with it. I’m just stuck there, and people are waiting. Or I’ll take that same little stack of napkins and fold them over and over, even though they’re already folded, and I know folks are trying to get by me.\n\nSo it’s not that I’m always making little movements all over the place, doctor. It’s more those odd things at church, where I get caught doing them and it embarrasses me.\n\nDoctor: When you start folding the napkins or holding the serving spoon, do you feel like you can't stop even if you want to, or does it feel more like you just happen to keep doing it without really trying to?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I just get caught in it, doctor. I don’t start out thinking, “I have to fold these napkins and I can’t stop,” exactly. It’s not like I’m choosing to do it over and over, but it’s not like I’m fully deciding either.\n\nLike at the potluck table, I’ll pick up the napkins because I’m supposed to be helping, and then I’ll fold that same little stack again, and then maybe again, and I know people are there waiting or trying to reach around me. Or with the serving spoon, I’ll have it up in my hand, ready to put food on somebody’s plate, and then I just sort of stand there not moving on. I can feel that I ought to stop or go to the next thing, but I don’t right away.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say it feels like something bad will happen if I stop. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more like I don’t realize it right away, doctor. I wouldn’t say my hands are locked or that I physically can’t put the spoon down. It’s not like my arm won’t obey at all. It’s just… I get stuck there before I notice what I’m doing.

That’s really why I came in, because at church I keep doing these odd things by the potluck table. I’ll be standing there with the serving spoon held up, and people are waiting for me to serve the food, and I’m just not moving along like I should. Or I’ll take the same little stack of napkins and fold them, then fuss with them again, like they still need fixing, even though they don’t. Then after a bit I realize, “Oh, I’m doing it again,” and I feel embarrassed because everybody’s waiting on me.

So I don’t think it’s that I physically can’t stop. It’s more that I don’t catch myself until it’s already gone on too long.

I’ll be helping, or trying to help, and then I’m just standing there with the serving spoon held up, like I’m about to serve something, but I don’t move on. People are waiting with their plates, and I know I’m in the way, but I sort of stay there too long.\n\nAnd the napkins too — I’ll take the same little stack and fold them again and again, like I’m doing something useful, but really I’m just stuck on it while folks are trying to get around me. That’s the embarrassing part. It’s not that I’m trying to act odd. I just find myself doing it, and then I realize people are noticing. That’s mostly why I came.\n\nDoctor: When you're holding the serving spoon or folding the napkins and you feel stuck, are you aware of what's going on around you, or does it feel like you're spaced out or not fully present?\n\nPatient: I think I’m aware, doctor… at least some of the time. It’s not like I’m completely gone or asleep or anything like that. I can see the people there with their plates, and I know I’m at the church potluck table, and I know I’m supposed to be helping. That’s what makes it so embarrassing, because I’m not unaware that folks are waiting on me.\n\nIt’s just that I get stuck in the action. Like I’ll be holding the serving spoon up, just sort of paused there, and I know I should either serve the food or put the spoon down, but I don’t move right away. Or with the napkins, I’ll be folding the same little stack over and over, and part of me knows, “You already did that,” but I keep doing it anyway. I can tell people are trying to get around me, and then I feel ashamed because I know it must look odd.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’m fully spaced out. It’s more like I’m there, but I’m not moving along the way I’m supposed to.\n\nDoctor: Doctor: Outside of church, have you ever felt driven to repeat certain actions over and over because you felt like something bad would happen if you didn’t, or because it just felt like you had to do it a certain way?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so, doctor. Not outside of church, not in that way you’re saying. I don’t have a thought like, “If I don’t do this three times something bad will happen,” or anything like that. At least I haven’t noticed that.\n\nIt’s more like, when it happens at the church table, I just get stuck doing it or holding still too long. But at home, I can’t say I’m driven to repeat things because I’m scared something will happen. I mean, I may check myself with ordinary things like anyone does, but not over and over because I feel I have to do it just right. I don’t really have an example of that.\n\nSo I guess the answer is no, not that I know of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself making small, repeated movements with your hands, face, or other parts of your body when you're not at church, things you can't quite control even if you try?\n\nPatient: Well… not really like my face twitching or my hands doing little movements when I’m just sitting at home, if that’s what you mean. I can’t say I’ve noticed that happening away from church.\n\nThe thing I keep trying to explain is more what happens there, at the church potluck table. I’ll be standing there with the serving spoon up in my hand, like I’m fixing to serve somebody, and then I just don’t go on with it. I’m just stuck there, and people are waiting. Or I’ll take that same little stack of napkins and fold them over and over, even though they’re already folded, and I know folks are trying to get by me.\n\nSo it’s not that I’m always making little movements all over the place, doctor. It’s more those odd things at church, where I get caught doing them and it embarrasses me.\n\nDoctor: When you start folding the napkins or holding the serving spoon, do you feel like you can't stop even if you want to, or does it feel more like you just happen to keep doing it without really trying to?\n\nPatient: It feels more like I just get caught in it, doctor. I don’t start out thinking, “I have to fold these napkins and I can’t stop,” exactly. It’s not like I’m choosing to do it over and over, but it’s not like I’m fully deciding either.\n\nLike at the potluck table, I’ll pick up the napkins because I’m supposed to be helping, and then I’ll fold that same little stack again, and then maybe again, and I know people are there waiting or trying to reach around me. Or with the serving spoon, I’ll have it up in my hand, ready to put food on somebody’s plate, and then I just sort of stand there not moving on. I can feel that I ought to stop or go to the next thing, but I don’t right away.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say it feels like something bad will happen if I stop. It’s more that I happen to keep doing it, or I freeze there, and then I notice everybody waiting and I feel foolish.\n\nDoctor: When you start folding the napkins or holding the serving spoon, do you feel an internal pressure or a strong need to keep doing it, even if you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a strong need, doctor. It’s not like there’s a voice in me saying, “Keep folding those napkins,” or “Don’t put that spoon down.” It’s more… I get stuck in the middle of what I’m doing.\n\nLike at church, when I’m by the potluck table, I’ll be holding the serving spoon up, ready to put something on somebody’s plate, and then I just stand there. I can see the person waiting, and I know I’m supposed to move, but I don’t move right away. Or with the napkins, I’ll have the same little stack in my hands and I’ll fold them over and over, even though there’s no reason to keep doing it. People are trying to get their food or get around me, and I know I’m acting odd, but I sort of keep on or freeze there.\n\nSo it isn’t exactly pressure like I have to do it to feel better. It’s more like my body or my mind doesn’t go on to the next step the way it should, and then I feel embarrassed because everybody is waiting on me.\n\nDoctor: Before you start holding the spoon up or folding the napkins over and over, do you ever feel a sudden, strong urge or impulse that you can't control, like something inside is pushing you to start the action even if you don't want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it starts like a sudden urge, no. Not like I’m sitting there and then all at once something inside shoves me to pick up the spoon or grab the napkins. It’s more that I’m already there at church, trying to help at the potluck table, and then I end up doing something odd.\n\nLike I’ll have the serving spoon in my hand, and I’m supposed to be putting food on somebody’s plate, and instead I just hold it up there and don’t move on. Or I’ll get hold of the same little stack of napkins and fold them again and again, even though they’re already folded, and people are waiting on me or trying to get by.\n\nSo before it starts, I can’t really say I feel some big impulse I can’t control. It’s more like I slip into it, and then I notice I’m standing there or repeating it, and by then I feel embarrassed because folks are looking and waiting.\n\nDoctor: Once you start holding the serving spoon or folding the napkins, do you feel like you physically can't stop, or is it more that you don't realize you should stop until after you've already done it several times?\n\nPatient: It’s more like I don’t realize it right away, doctor. I wouldn’t say my hands are locked or that I physically can’t put the spoon down. It’s not like my arm won’t obey at all. It’s just… I get stuck there before I notice what I’m doing.\n\nThat’s really why I came in, because at church I keep doing these odd things by the potluck table. I’ll be standing there with the serving spoon held up, and people are waiting for me to serve the food, and I’m just not moving along like I should. Or I’ll take the same little stack of napkins and fold them, then fuss with them again, like they still need fixing, even though they don’t. Then after a bit I realize, “Oh, I’m doing it again,” and I feel embarrassed because everybody’s waiting on me.\n\nSo I don’t think it’s that I physically can’t stop. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I would call it spacing out exactly, doctor. I mean, I’m there, I can see the people at the potluck table, and I know they’re waiting on me, but it’s like I don’t move along with what I’m supposed to be doing.

So I’m not gone completely, if that’s what you mean. I know where I am. But in those moments I seem to lose the thread of what I’m supposed to do next, and then when I notice, I feel awful embarrassed because folks are looking at me and waiting.

Or with the serving spoon, I’ll have it up in my hand, ready to put food on somebody’s plate, and then I just sort of stand there not moving on. I can feel that I ought to stop or go to the next thing, but I don’t right away.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say it feels like something bad will happen if I stop. It’s more that I happen to keep doing it, or I freeze there, and then I notice everybody waiting and I feel foolish.\n\nDoctor: When you start folding the napkins or holding the serving spoon, do you feel an internal pressure or a strong need to keep doing it, even if you don’t want to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a strong need, doctor. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think so, doctor. I don’t really feel like I’m outside my body watching myself, not that I’ve noticed. I’m still me, standing right there by the table, holding the spoon or messing with the napkins. I can see my hands, I can see the people, and I know I’m there in the church hall.

It’s more like I get caught in the action and don’t move on like I should. Then when I come to it a little more, I feel embarrassed, because I know people must be wondering why I’m just standing there or why I’m folding the same napkins again. But I wouldn’t say I feel disconnected from myself. It’s not like I’m floating above or watching some other lady do it. It’s still me, just… stuck and slow to notice.

Not like I’m sitting there and then all at once something inside shoves me to pick up the spoon or grab the napkins. Or I’ll get hold of the same little stack of napkins and fold them again and again, even though they’re already folded, and people are waiting on me or trying to get by.\n\nSo before it starts, I can’t really say I feel some big impulse I can’t control. It’s more like I slip into it, and then I notice I’m standing there or repeating it, and by then I feel embarrassed because folks are looking and waiting.\n\nDoctor: Once you start holding the serving spoon or folding the napkins, do you feel like you physically can't stop, or is it more that you don't realize you should stop until after you've already done it several times?\n\nPatient: It’s more like I don’t realize it right away, doctor.
